Add or subtract:

o a) (4x)/7 + (3x)/7
o b) x/3 + x/6
o c) (3a)/4 - a/8
o d) (x + 2)/5 + (x - 1)/5

If x = 4, find:

WEEK 2: SIMPLE EQUATIONS

TOPIC: Meaning of Algebraic Equations; Differences Between Expressions and Equations; Solving Simple Algebraic Equations
CONTENT:
ALGEBRAIC EQUATIONS

Definition: An algebraic equation is a mathematical statement showing that two algebraic expressions are equal, connected by an equals sign (=).
Parts of an Equation:

Left-Hand Side (LHS): Expression before the = sign
Right-Hand Side (RHS): Expression after the = sign
Equals Sign (=): Shows both sides have the same value
Example 1: In the equation 3x + 5 = 11 LHS = 3x + 5
RHS = 11
The equation states that 3x + 5 equals 11

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN EXPRESSIONS AND EQUATIONS

Algebraic ExpressionAlgebraic EquationNo equals sign (=)Has equals sign (=)Cannot be solvedCan be solvedCan only be simplifiedHas a solution/answerExamples: 3x + 5, 2a - 7, x² + 3xExamples: 3x + 5 = 11, 2a - 7 = 15, x² = 9Example 2: Identify as expression or equation:

5x + 7 → Expression (no = sign)
5x + 7 = 22 → Equation (has = sign)
3a - 2b → Expression
3a - 2b = 12 → Equation
x² + 4x - 5 → Expression
x² + 4x = 5 → Equation

MEANING OF SOLVING AN EQUATION

To solve an equation means to find the value of the unknown variable that makes the equation true.
Solution/Root: The value that satisfies the equation.
Example 3: Is x = 3 a solution to 2x + 5 = 11? Check by substitution:
LHS = 2(3) + 5 = 6 + 5 = 11
RHS = 11 LHS = RHS ✓

Yes, x = 3 is the solution
Example 4: Is a = 5 a solution to 3a - 7 = 10? Check:
LHS = 3(5) - 7 = 15 - 7 = 8
RHS = 10 LHS ≠ RHS ✗

No, a = 5 is not the solution

SOLVING SIMPLE LINEAR EQUATIONS

Linear Equation: An equation where the highest power of the variable is 1. General form: ax + b = c
Rules for Solving:

Addition Property: Add the same number to both sides
Subtraction Property: Subtract the same number from both sides
Multiplication Property: Multiply both sides by the same number
Division Property: Divide both sides by the same number (not zero)

Goal: Isolate the variable on one side of the equation.

Type 1: x + a = b
Example 5: Solve: x + 7 = 15 Solution:
x + 7 = 15
Subtract 7 from both sides: x + 7 - 7 = 15 - 7
x = 8

Check: 8 + 7 = 15 ✓
Example 6: Solve: y + 12 = 20 Solution:
y + 12 = 20
y = 20 - 12
y = 8

Example 7: Solve: a + 3.5 = 10
Solution:
a = 10 - 3.5
a = 6.5

Type 2: x - a = b
Example 8: Solve: x - 5 = 12 Solution:
x - 5 = 12
Add 5 to both sides:
x - 5 + 5 = 12 + 5
x = 17

Check: 17 - 5 = 12 ✓
Example 9: Solve: m - 8 = 15 Solution:
m = 15 + 8
m = 23
Example 10: Solve: y - 4.2 = 6.8 Solution:
y = 6.8 + 4.2
y = 11

Type 3: ax = b

Example 11: Solve: 5x = 30
Solution:
5x = 30
Divide both sides by 5: 5x/5 = 30/5
x = 6

Check: 5(6) = 30 ✓
Example 12: Solve: 7y = 42 Solution:
y = 42/7 y = 6
Example 13: Solve: 3a = 18

Solution: a = 18/3 a = 6

Type 4: x/a = b
Example 14: Solve: x/4 = 7 Solution:
x/4 = 7
Multiply both sides by 4: x/4 × 4 = 7 × 4
x = 28

Check: 28/4 = 7 ✓

Example 15: Solve: y/5 = 9
Solution:
y = 9 × 5
y = 45
Example 16: Solve: a/3 = 12 Solution:
a = 12 × 3
a = 36

Type 5: ax + b = c
Example 17: Solve: 3x + 5 = 20 Solution:
3x + 5 = 20
Subtract 5 from both sides: 3x = 20 - 5
3x = 15
Divide by 3:
x = 15/3 x = 5

Check: 3(5) + 5 = 15 + 5 = 20 ✓
Example 18: Solve: 5y - 7 = 18 Solution:
5y = 18 + 7
5y = 25
y = 25/5 y = 5
Example 19: Solve: 2a + 9 = 25
Solution:
2a = 25 - 9
2a = 16
a = 8
Example 20: Solve: 4x - 11 = 17 Solution:
4x = 17 + 11
4x = 28
x = 7

Type 6: ax - b = c
Example 21: Solve: 6x - 15 = 33 Solution:
6x = 33 + 15
6x = 48
x = 8
Example 22: Solve: 7y - 12 = 30 Solution:
7y = 30 + 12
7y = 42
y = 6

Type 7: Equations with x on both sides
Example 23: Solve: 5x + 3 = 2x + 18 Solution:
Collect x terms on left, constants on right:
5x - 2x = 18 - 3
3x = 15
x = 5

Check:
LHS = 5(5) + 3 = 28
RHS = 2(5) + 18 = 28 ✓
Example 24: Solve: 7y - 4 = 3y + 20 Solution:
7y - 3y = 20 + 4
4y = 24
y = 6
Example 25: Solve: 8a + 7 = 5a + 25 Solution:
8a - 5a = 25 - 7
3a = 18
a = 6
Example 26: Solve: 4x - 9 = x + 12 Solution:
4x - x = 12 + 9
3x = 21
x = 7

Type 8: Equations with brackets Example 27: Solve: 3(x + 4) = 24
Solution:
Expand bracket:
3x + 12 = 24
3x = 24 - 12
3x = 12
x = 4

Check: 3(4 + 4) = 3(8) = 24 ✓
Example 28: Solve: 2(y - 5) = 18 Solution:
2y - 10 = 18
2y = 18 + 10
2y = 28
y = 14
Example 29: Solve: 5(2a + 3) = 55 Solution:
10a + 15 = 55
10a = 55 - 15
10a = 40
a = 4
Example 30: Solve: 4(x - 3) + 5 = 21 Solution:
4x - 12 + 5 = 21
4x - 7 = 21
4x = 21 + 7
4x = 28
x = 7

Type 9: Equations with fractions
Example 31: Solve: x/3 + 5 = 11 Solution:
x/3 = 11 - 5
x/3 = 6
x = 6 × 3
x = 18
Example 32: Solve: (2x + 1)/3 = 7 Solution:
Multiply both sides by 3: 2x + 1 = 21
2x = 21 - 1
2x = 20
x = 10
Example 33: Solve: (y - 4)/5 = 3 Solution:
y - 4 = 3 × 5
y - 4 = 15
y = 19

Type 10: Equations with negative coefficients Example 34: Solve: -3x = 15
Solution: x = 15/(-3) x = -5

Check: -3(-5) = 15 ✓
Example 35: Solve: -2y + 7 = 15 Solution:
-2y = 15 - 7
-2y = 8
y = 8/(-2) y = -4
Example 36: Solve: 5 - 3x = 20

Solution:
-3x = 20 - 5
-3x = 15
x = -5

CHECKING SOLUTIONS

Always verify your solution by substituting back into the original equation.
Example 37: Solve and check: 4x - 7 = 13 Solution:
4x = 13 + 7
4x = 20
x = 5

Check:
LHS = 4(5) - 7 = 20 - 7 = 13 ✓
RHS = 13 ✓

WORD PROBLEMS LEADING TO SIMPLE EQUATIONS
Example 38: When 7 is added to a number, the result is 23. Find the number. Solution:
Let the number = x x + 7 = 23
x = 23 - 7
x = 16

The number is 16.
Example 39: A number multiplied by 5 gives 45. Find the number. Solution:
Let the number = x 5x = 45
x = 9

The number is 9.
Example 40: Three times a number, decreased by 8, equals 19. Find the number. Solution:
Let the number = x 3x - 8 = 19
3x = 27
x = 9

The number is 9.
Example 41: The sum of two consecutive numbers is 27. Find the numbers. Solution:
Let first number = x Second number = x + 1

x + (x + 1) = 27 2x + 1 = 27
2x = 26
x = 13

First number = 13 Second number = 14
Example 42: Mary is 5 years older than John. The sum of their ages is 35. Find their ages.

Solution:
Let John's age = x Mary's age = x + 5

x + (x + 5) = 35 2x + 5 = 35
2x = 30
x = 15

John = 15 years Mary = 20 years
Example 43: A rectangle's length is 3 times its width. If the perimeter is 48 cm, find the dimensions.
Solution:
Let width = x Length = 3x

Perimeter = 2(length + width) 48 = 2(3x + x)
48 = 2(4x)
48 = 8x
x = 6

Width = 6 cm Length = 18 cm
Example 44: A father is 4 times as old as his son. In 5 years, he will be 3 times as old. Find their current ages.
Solution:
Let son's current age = x Father's current age = 4x

In 5 years:
Son's age = x + 5 Father's age = 4x + 5

Equation: 4x + 5 = 3(x + 5) 4x + 5 = 3x + 15
4x - 3x = 15 - 5 x = 10

Son = 10 years Father = 40 years
Example 45: The cost of 5 pens and 3 books is ₦1,700. If each book costs ₦200, find the cost of each pen.
Solution:
Cost of 3 books = 3 × 200 = ₦600 Cost of 5 pens = 1,700 - 600 = ₦1,100

Let cost of 1 pen = x
5x = 1,100
x = 220

Each pen costs ₦220.

WEEK 3: LINEAR INEQUALITIES I

TOPIC: Definition of Linear Inequalities; Introduction to Symbols and Basic Properties

CONTENT:
INTRODUCTION TO INEQUALITIES

Definition: An inequality is a mathematical statement that compares two expressions using inequality symbols instead of an equals sign.
Equality vs Inequality:

Equation: 3x + 5 = 11 (both sides are equal)
Inequality: 3x + 5 > 11 (one side is greater than the other)

INEQUALITY SYMBOLS

SymbolMeaningExampleReading>Greater thanx > 5x is greater than 5<Less thanx < 3x is less than 3≥Greater than or equal tox ≥ 7x is greater than or equal to 7≤Less than or equal tox ≤ 4x is less than or equal to 4≠Not equal tox ≠ 2x is not equal to 2

UNDERSTANDING INEQUALITY SYMBOLS

Example 1: x > 5

This means x can be any value greater than 5 Examples: 6, 7, 10, 5.1, 100, etc.
NOT: 5 (x must be greater than, not equal to 5)

Example 2: y < 3
This means y can be any value less than 3 Examples: 2, 1, 0, -5, 2.9, etc.
NOT: 3 (y must be less than, not equal to 3)

Example 3: a ≥ 7

This means a can be 7 or any value greater than 7 Examples: 7, 8, 9, 10, 7.5, 100, etc.
Example 4: b ≤ 4

This means b can be 4 or any value less than 4 Examples: 4, 3, 2, 1, 0, -10, 3.9, etc.

GRAPHICAL REPRESENTATION ON NUMBER LINE

Open Circle (○): Used for > and < (value not included) Closed/Filled Circle (●): Used for ≥ and ≤ (value included)
Example 5: Represent x > 3 on a number line

————————→  0 1 2 3 4 5 6

Open circle at 3, arrow to the right

Example 6: Represent x < 2 on a number line

←————————○
-2 -1  0  1  2 3  4

Open circle at 2, arrow to the left

Example 7: Represent x ≥ 4 on a number line
————————→ 0 1 2 3 4 5 6

Closed circle at 4, arrow to the right

Example 8: Represent x ≤ 1 on a number line

←————————●
-2 -1  0  1  2 3  4

Closed circle at 1, arrow to the left

Example 9: Represent -2 < x ≤ 3

————————●
-3 -2 -1 0  1 2  3  4

Open circle at -2, closed circle at 3
x is between -2 and 3 (not including -2, but including 3)

SOLUTION SETS OF INEQUALITIES Solution Set: All values that satisfy the inequality. Example 10: List three values that satisfy x > 5
Examples: 6, 7, 10
(Any value greater than 5)
Example 11: List three values that satisfy y ≤ 2 Examples: 2, 1, 0
(Any value less than or equal to 2)

Example 12: Which of the following satisfy x ≥ 3?
Values: 2, 3, 4, 5, 1

Check:
2: 2 ≥ 3? No ✗
3: 3 ≥ 3? Yes ✓
4: 4 ≥ 3? Yes ✓
5: 5 ≥ 3? Yes ✓
1: 1 ≥ 3? No ✗

Answer: 3, 4, 5

PROPERTIES OF INEQUALITIES

Property 1: Addition Property If a > b, then a + c > b + c

Example 13:

If x > 5, then x + 3 > 5 + 3
So x + 3 > 8

Property 2: Subtraction Property If a > b, then a - c > b - c

Example 14:

If y < 10, then y - 4 < 10 - 4
So y - 4 < 6

Property 3: Multiplication Property (Positive Number) If a > b and c > 0, then ac > bc

Example 15:

If x > 3, then 2x > 2(3) So 2x > 6
Property 4: Division Property (Positive Number) If a > b and c > 0, then a/c > b/c
Example 16:

If 3y < 12, then 3y/3 < 12/3 So y < 4
Property 5: Multiplication/Division by Negative Number CRITICAL: When multiplying or dividing by a negative number, reverse the inequality sign.
Example 17:

If x > 5, then -2x < -2(5) [Note: sign reversed!] So -2x < -10
Example 18:

If -3y < 15, then -3y/(-3) > 15/(-3) [Sign reversed!] So y > -5
Example 19:

If -x > 7, multiply by -1:
-x × (-1) < 7 × (-1) [Sign reversed!]
x < -7

COMPARING INEQUALITY STATEMENTS
Example 20: Which is stronger: x > 5 or x ≥ 5? x > 5 is stronger (more restrictive)
Because x ≥ 5 includes more values (includes 5 itself)

Example 21: True or False?

If x > 3, then x > 2 True ✓ (anything greater than 3 is also greater than 2)
If x < 5, then x < 10 True ✓
If x ≤ 4, then x < 4 False ✗ (≤ 4 includes 4, but < 4 doesn't)
If x ≥ 7, then x = 7 False ✗ (≥ 7 includes many values, not just 7)

DOUBLE INEQUALITIES

Definition: An inequality with two inequality signs.

Example 22: 2 < x < 7

This means: x > 2 AND x < 7
x is between 2 and 7 (not including 2 and 7)
Examples: 3, 4, 5, 6, 2.1, 6.9

Example 23: -3 ≤ y ≤ 5

This means: y ≥ -3 AND y ≤ 5
y is between -3 and 5 (including both -3 and 5)
Examples: -3, -2, 0, 3, 5

Example 24: Represent -1 < x ≤ 4 on a number line

————————●
-2 -1  0  1  2 3  4  5

Open circle at -1, closed circle at 4

TRANSLATING WORDS TO INEQUALITIES

Example 25: Write as inequalities:

x is greater than 10 → x > 10
y is at most 5 → y ≤ 5
a is at least 8 → a ≥ 8
b is less than 12 → b < 12
c is no more than 15 → c ≤ 15
d is no less than 20 → d ≥ 20
e is between 3 and 7 → 3 < e < 7

Example 26: Write in words:

x > 15 → x is greater than 15
y ≤ 20 → y is less than or equal to 20 OR y is at most 20
a ≥ 30 → a is greater than or equal to 30 OR a is at least 30
5 < b < 10 → b is between 5 and 10

REAL-LIFE SITUATIONS WITH INEQUALITIES

Example 27: Age Restrictions

"You must be at least 18 years old to vote" If age = a, then: a ≥ 18
Example 28: Speed Limits

"Speed must not exceed 100 km/h" If speed = s, then: s ≤ 100
Example 29: Temperature

"Temperature should be kept below 5°C" If temperature = t, then: t < 5
Example 30: Weight Limits

"Elevator capacity is at most 1000 kg" If weight = w, then: w ≤ 1000
Example 31: Height Requirements

"Children taller than 1.2m can ride" If height = h, then: h > 1.2
Example 32: Price Range

"Budget is between ₦5,000 and ₦10,000" If price = p, then: 5,000 ≤ p ≤ 10,000

SIMPLE SOLVING (Introduction)
Example 33: Solve: x + 3 > 8 Solution:
x + 3 > 8
Subtract 3 from both sides: x > 8 - 3
x > 5

Number line:
————————→  0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7
Example 34: Solve: y - 5 < 12

Solution:
y - 5 < 12
Add 5 to both sides: y < 12 + 5
y < 17
Example 35: Solve: 2x ≥ 14 Solution:
2x ≥ 14
Divide both sides by 2: x ≥ 14/2
x ≥ 7
Example 36: Solve: x/3 ≤ 5

Solution:
x/3 ≤ 5
Multiply both sides by 3: x ≤ 5 × 3
x ≤ 15
Example 37: Solve: -x > 4 Solution:
-x > 4
Multiply by -1 (reverse sign!): x < -4

WEEK 4: LINEAR INEQUALITIES II

TOPIC: Word Problems Leading to Simple Inequalities in One Variable; Solving and Interpreting Results
CONTENT:
SOLVING LINEAR INEQUALITIES

Linear Inequality: An inequality where the highest power of the variable is 1.

General Forms:

ax + b > c
ax + b < c
ax + b ≥ c
ax + b ≤ c

Steps to Solve:

Simplify both sides if necessary
Collect variable terms on one side, constants on the other
Isolate the variable
Remember: Reverse inequality sign when multiplying/dividing by negative number
Check solution
Represent on number line

Type 1: x + a > b
Example 1: Solve: x + 7 > 15 Solution:
x + 7 > 15
x > 15 - 7
x > 8
Number line:
————————→    5 6 7 8 9 10 11

Solution set: {x: x > 8} or x ∈ (8, ∞)
Example 2: Solve: y + 5 ≤ 12

Solution:
y ≤ 12 - 5
y ≤ 7

Number line:
←————————● 3 4 5 6 7 8 9

Type 2: x - a < b
Example 3: Solve: x - 9 < 15 Solution:
x < 15 + 9
x < 24
Example 4: Solve: y - 4 ≥ 11 Solution:
y ≥ 11 + 4
y ≥ 15

Type 3: ax > b
Example 5: Solve: 5x > 30

Solution:
x > 30/5 x > 6

Check: Try x = 7 5(7) = 35 > 30 ✓
Example 6: Solve: 3y ≤ 21

Solution: y ≤ 21/3 y ≤ 7

Type 4: x/a < b
Example 7: Solve: x/4 < 6 Solution:
x < 6 × 4
x < 24
Example 8: Solve: y/3 ≥ 5 Solution:
y ≥ 5 × 3
y ≥ 15

Type 5: ax + b > c

Example 9: Solve: 3x + 5 > 20
Solution:
3x > 20 - 5
3x > 15
x > 15/3 x > 5

Check: Try x = 6 3(6) + 5 = 23 > 20 ✓
Example 10: Solve: 2y - 7 ≤ 13

Solution:
2y ≤ 13 + 7
2y ≤ 20
y ≤ 10
Example 11: Solve: 5a + 8 < 33 Solution:
5a < 33 - 8
5a < 25
a < 5
Example 12: Solve: 4x - 9 ≥ 15 Solution:
4x ≥ 15 + 9
4x ≥ 24
x ≥ 6

Type 6: Inequalities with Variable on Both Sides Example 13: Solve: 5x + 3 > 2x + 18
Solution:
5x - 2x > 18 - 3
3x > 15
x > 5

Check: Try x = 6 LHS: 5(6) + 3 = 33
RHS: 2(6) + 18 = 30
33 > 30 ✓
Example 14: Solve: 7y - 4 ≤ 3y + 20 Solution:
7y - 3y ≤ 20 + 4
4y ≤ 24
y ≤ 6
Example 15: Solve: 8a + 7 < 5a + 28 Solution:
8a - 5a < 28 - 7
3a < 21
a < 7

Type 7: Inequalities with Brackets Example 16: Solve: 3(x + 4) > 24
Solution:
3x + 12 > 24
3x > 24 - 12
3x > 12
x > 4
Example 17: Solve: 2(y - 5) ≤ 18

Solution:
2y - 10 ≤ 18
2y ≤ 18 + 10
2y ≤ 28
y ≤ 14
Example 18: Solve: 5(2a + 3) ≥ 65 Solution:
10a + 15 ≥ 65
10a ≥ 50
a ≥ 5

Type 8: Negative Coefficients (CRITICAL) Example 19: Solve: -3x > 12
Solution:
Divide by -3 (REVERSE THE SIGN!): x < 12/(-3)
x < -4

Number line:
←————————○
-7 -6 -5 -4 -3 -2 -1
Example 20: Solve: -2y ≤ 10 Solution:
Divide by -2 (reverse sign): y ≥ 10/(-2)
y ≥ -5
Example 21: Solve: -5a + 7 > 22

Solution:
-5a > 22 - 7
-5a > 15
Divide by -5 (reverse sign): a < 15/(-5)
a < -3
Example 22: Solve: 8 - 4x < 20 Solution:
-4x < 20 - 8
-4x < 12
Divide by -4 (reverse sign): x > 12/(-4)
x > -3

Type 9: Fractions
Example 23: Solve: (x + 5)/3 > 4 Solution:
Multiply both sides by 3: x + 5 > 12
x > 12 - 5
x > 7
Example 24: Solve: (2y - 3)/5 ≤ 3 Solution:
2y - 3 ≤ 15
2y ≤ 18
y ≤ 9

Type 10: Double Inequalities
Example 25: Solve: 5 < 2x + 1 < 13 Solution:
Split into two inequalities:

Part 1: 5 < 2x + 1
4 < 2x
2 < x or x > 2

Part 2: 2x + 1 < 13
2x < 12
x < 6
Combined: 2 < x < 6 Number line:
○————————○ 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7

Solution: {x: 2 < x < 6}
Example 26: Solve: -1 ≤ 3x - 4 ≤ 8 Solution:
Part 1: -1 ≤ 3x - 4
3 ≤ 3x
1 ≤ x or x ≥ 1

Part 2: 3x - 4 ≤ 8
3x ≤ 12
x ≤ 4

Combined: 1 ≤ x ≤ 4 Solution: {x: 1 ≤ x ≤ 4}

WORD PROBLEMS WITH INEQUALITIES
Example 27: A number increased by 15 is greater than 42. Find the possible values. Solution:
Let the number = x x + 15 > 42
x > 42 - 15
x > 27

Answer: The number must be greater than 27.
Example 28: Five times a number is at most 45. Find the possible values. Solution:
Let the number = x 5x ≤ 45
x ≤ 9

Answer: The number can be at most 9.
Example 29: Three times a number, decreased by 8, is less than 19. Find possible values. Solution:
Let the number = x 3x - 8 < 19
3x < 27
x < 9

Answer: The number must be less than 9.
Example 30: Twice a number plus 7 is at least 23. What are the possible values? Solution:
Let the number = x 2x + 7 ≥ 23
2x ≥ 16
x ≥ 8

Answer: The number must be at least 8.

Age Problems:
Example 31: John's age plus 5 is more than 20. How old can John be? Solution:
Let John's age = x x + 5 > 20
x > 15

Answer: John must be older than 15 years.

Example 32: Mary is at least 3 years older than twice her brother's age. If her brother is 7, how old can Mary be?
Solution:
Brother's age = 7
Twice brother's age = 2 × 7 = 14 Let Mary's age = x
x ≥ 14 + 3
x ≥ 17

Answer: Mary must be at least 17 years old.

Money Problems:

Example 33: You have ₦5,000. After buying books that cost ₦350 each, you want to have at least ₦2,000 left. How many books can you buy?
Solution:
Let number of books = x Cost of books = 350x Money left = 5,000 - 350x

5,000 - 350x ≥ 2,000
-350x ≥ 2,000 - 5,000
-350x ≥ -3,000
Divide by -350 (reverse sign):
x ≤ -3,000/(-350)
x ≤ 8.57

Answer: You can buy at most 8 books.

Example 34: A taxi charges ₦200 plus ₦50 per kilometer. If you have at most ₦1,500, how far can you travel?
Solution:
Let distance = x km Cost = 200 + 50x

200 + 50x ≤ 1,500
50x ≤ 1,300
x ≤ 26

Answer: You can travel at most 26 km.

Perimeter Problems:

Example 35: A rectangle's length is 3 times its width. If the perimeter must be less than 80 cm, find possible widths.
Solution:
Let width = x Length = 3x
Perimeter = 2(length + width) = 2(3x + x) = 8x

8x < 80
x < 10

Answer: Width must be less than 10 cm.

Example 36: An isosceles triangle has equal sides of length x cm and base of 8 cm. If perimeter is at least 26 cm, find possible values of x.
Solution:
Perimeter = x + x + 8 = 2x + 8

2x + 8 ≥ 26
2x ≥ 18
x ≥ 9

Answer: Each equal side must be at least 9 cm.

Grade/Score Problems:

Example 37: To pass a test, you need an average of at least 60% on 5 tests. If your first 4 scores are 55, 62, 58, and 65, what must you score on the 5th test?
Solution:
Let 5th score = x
Average = (55 + 62 + 58 + 65 + x)/5

(55 + 62 + 58 + 65 + x)/5 ≥ 60
(240 + x)/5 ≥ 60
240 + x ≥ 300
x ≥ 60

Answer: You must score at least 60 on the 5th test.

Temperature Problems:

Example 38: For a science experiment, temperature must be kept between 15°C and 25°C. Currently it's 18°C. If temperature rises by 2°C every hour, for how long can the experiment continue?
Solution:
Let hours = x
Final temperature = 18 + 2x Must satisfy: 15 ≤ 18 + 2x ≤ 25
Upper limit: 18 + 2x ≤ 25 2x ≤ 7
x ≤ 3.5

Answer: Experiment can continue for at most 3.5 hours (or 3 hours 30 minutes).

Weight/Capacity Problems:

Example 39: An elevator can carry at most 800 kg. There are already 5 people averaging 65 kg each. What's the maximum weight of additional person(s) that can enter?
Solution:
Current weight = 5 × 65 = 325 kg Let additional weight = x

325 + x ≤ 800
x ≤ 475

Answer: Additional weight can be at most 475 kg.

Profit/Loss Problems:

Example 40: A trader buys items for ₦250 each. If he must make at least ₦50 profit on each, what's the minimum selling price?
Solution:
Cost price = ₦250 Let selling price = x Profit = x - 250

x - 250 ≥ 50
x ≥ 300

Answer: Minimum selling price is ₦300.
Example 41: A company's monthly expenses are ₦150,000. If they sell products at ₦800 each, how many must they sell to make profit?
Solution:
Let number of products = x Revenue = 800x
For profit: Revenue > Expenses

800x > 150,000
x > 187.5

Answer: They must sell at least 188 products.

INTERPRETING SOLUTIONS
Example 42: After solving x > 5, interpret: Interpretation:
x can be any value greater than 5
x = 5 is NOT included
- Examples: 5.1, 6, 7, 10, 100, etc.
Graphically: Open circle at 5, arrow to right
In real context: "More than 5"
Example 43: After solving y ≤ 12, interpret: Interpretation:
y can be 12 or any value less than 12
y = 12 IS included
- Examples: 12, 11, 10, 0, -5, etc.
Graphically: Closed circle at 12, arrow to left
In real context: "At most 12" or "12 or fewer"
Example 44: After solving 3 < x ≤ 8, interpret:

Interpretation:
x is between 3 and 8
x = 3 is NOT included (open circle)
x = 8 IS included (closed circle)
- Examples: 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 3.1, 7.9
In real context: "More than 3 but not more than 8"

WEEK 6: GRAPHS I

TOPIC: Graphical Representation; Graphs of Cartesian Plane; Introduction to Graphs of Linear Equations in Two Variables
CONTENT:
INTRODUCTION TO GRAPHS

Definition: A graph is a visual representation of data or mathematical relationships using points, lines, or curves on a coordinate system.
Why Use Graphs?

Visual representation of data
Easy to understand patterns and trends
Compare information quickly
Show relationships between variables
Make predictions

Types of Graphs:

Bar graphs
Line graphs
Pie charts
Pictographs
Scatter plots
Linear graphs (focus of this topic)

THE CARTESIAN PLANE (COORDINATE SYSTEM)

Definition: The Cartesian plane (also called coordinate plane or xy-plane) is a two-dimensional surface formed by two perpendicular number lines.
Components:
The x-axis (Horizontal axis)

←————————————————→
x-axis

Runs left to right
Positive direction: right
Negative direction: left

The y-axis (Vertical axis)

↑
|
xis
|
↓

Runs up and down
Positive direction: up
Negative direction: down

Origin (O)

Point where x-axis and y-axis intersect
Coordinates: (0, 0)
The reference point

Complete Cartesian Plane:

y-axis
↑
|
|	I
|
←—————————O—————————→ x-axis
|
|	IV
|
↓

THE FOUR QUADRANTS

QuadrantPositionx-valuey-valueExample PointI (First)Top rightPositive (+)Positive (+)(3, 4)II (Second)Top leftNegative (-)Positive (+)(-2, 5)III (Third)Bottom leftNegative (-)Negative (-)(-4, -3)IV (Fourth)Bottom rightPositive (+)Negative (-)(5, -2)

COORDINATES (ORDERED PAIRS)

Definition: A coordinate is an ordered pair (x, y) that locates a point on the Cartesian plane.

Format: (x, y)

x-coordinate (abscissa): Distance from y-axis (horizontal)
y-coordinate (ordinate): Distance from x-axis (vertical)

Important: Order matters! (3, 4) ≠ (4, 3)

PLOTTING POINTS

Steps to Plot a Point (x, y):

Start at the origin (0, 0)
Move x units along x-axis (right if +, left if -)
From there, move y units parallel to y-axis (up if +, down if -)
Mark the point

LINEAR EQUATIONS IN TWO VARIABLES

Definition: A linear equation in two variables is an equation that can be written in the form: ax + by = c
Where a, b, c are constants, and x, y are variables.

Examples:

a) x + y = 5
b) 2x + 3y = 12
y = 2x + 1
x - y = 4
y = 3x

Characteristics:

Highest power of variables is 1
Graph is always a straight line
Has infinitely many solutions (ordered pairs)

FINDING SOLUTIONS TO LINEAR EQUATIONS Solution: Any ordered pair (x, y) that satisfies the equation. Example 6: Is (2, 3) a solution to x + y = 5?
Check:
x + y = 5
2 + 3 = 5 ✓

Yes, (2, 3) is a solution.

Example 7: Is (4, 1) a solution to x + y = 5?
Check:
x + y = 5
4 + 1 = 5 ✓

Yes, (4, 1) is a solution.
Example 8: Is (3, 1) a solution to x + y = 5? Check:
x + y = 5
3 + 1 = 4 ✗

No, (3, 1) is not a solution.

FINDING MULTIPLE SOLUTIONS

Method: Choose values for one variable, solve for the other.

Example 9: Find 5 solutions to x + y = 6

Create a table:

xy = 6 - x(x, y)06 - 0 = 6(0, 6)16 - 1 = 5(1, 5)26 - 2 = 4(2, 4)36 - 3 = 3(3, 3)46 - 4 = 2(4, 2)Solutions: (0, 6), (1, 5), (2, 4), (3, 3), (4, 2)

Example 10: Find 4 solutions to y = 2x
xy = 2x(x, y)02(0) = 0(0, 0)12(1) = 2(1, 2)22(2) = 4(2, 4)32(3) = 6(3, 6)
Solutions: (0, 0), (1, 2), (2, 4), (3, 6)

Example 11: Find 5 solutions to 2x + y = 8

xy = 8 - 2x(x, y)08 - 2(0) = 8(0, 8)18 - 2(1) = 6(1, 6)28 - 2(2) = 4(2, 4)38 - 2(3) = 2(3, 2)48 - 2(4) = 0(4, 0)Solutions: (0, 8), (1, 6), (2, 4), (3, 2), (4, 0)

INTRODUCTION TO GRAPHING LINEAR EQUATIONS Key Concept: All solutions to a linear equation lie on a straight line. Example 12: Graph x + y = 5

INTERCEPTS

x-intercept: Point where line crosses x-axis (y = 0) y-intercept: Point where line crosses y-axis (x = 0)
Example 13: Find intercepts of x + y = 6

x-intercept (let y = 0):

x + 0 = 6
x = 6
x-intercept: (6, 0)

y-intercept (let x = 0):
0 + y = 6
y = 6
y-intercept: (0, 6)

Example 14: Find intercepts of 2x + 3y = 12

x-intercept:

2x + 3(0) = 12
2x = 12
x = 6
x-intercept: (6, 0)

y-intercept:

2(0) + 3y = 12
3y = 12
y = 4
y-intercept: (0, 4)

SLOPE (GRADIENT)

Definition: The slope/gradient of a line measures its steepness.

Formula:

Slope (m) = Change in y / Change in x
= (y₂ - y₁) / (x₂ - x₁)
= Rise / Run
Example 15: Find slope of line through (1, 2) and (3, 6) m = (y₂ - y₁) / (x₂ - x₁)
= (6 - 2) / (3 - 1)
= 4 / 2
= 2

Slope = 2

Types of Slopes:

Positive slope: / (rises left to right) Negative slope: \ (falls left to right) Zero slope: —	(horizontal line) Undefined slope: | (vertical line)

REVIEW OF GRAPHING LINEAR EQUATIONS

Steps to Graph a Linear Equation:

Find at least 3 solutions (ordered pairs)
Create a table of values
Plot the points on Cartesian plane
Draw a straight line through points
Label the line with equation
Mark intercepts if required

GRAPHING LINEAR EQUATIONS IN STANDARD FORM

3x = 12

WEEK 9: PLANE FIGURES/SHAPES

TOPIC: Identifying Plane Shapes in the Environment; Properties of Plane Shapes (Square, Rectangle, Parallelogram, Rhombus, Kite)
CONTENT:
INTRODUCTION TO PLANE FIGURES

Definition: A plane figure (or plane shape) is a flat, two-dimensional shape that lies entirely in one plane. It has length and width but no thickness.
Characteristics:

Two-dimensional (2D)
Flat surface
Has area and perimeter
Cannot be held or have volume

Examples of Plane Figures:

Triangles
Quadrilaterals (4-sided shapes)
Circles
Polygons

Focus: Quadrilaterals (4-sided polygons)

QUADRILATERALS

Definition: A quadrilateral is a polygon with four sides, four angles, and four vertices.

Properties of ALL Quadrilaterals:

Four sides
Four angles
Four vertices (corners)
Sum of interior angles = 360°
Two diagonals

Types of Quadrilaterals:

Square
Rectangle
Parallelogram
Rhombus
Trapezium
Kite

SQUARE

Definition: A square is a quadrilateral with all sides equal and all angles equal to 90°.

Properties:

All sides are equal (AB = BC = CD = DA)
All angles are 90° (right angles)
Opposite sides are parallel
Diagonals are equal in length
Diagonals bisect each other at 90°
Diagonals bisect the angles (divide into 45° each)
Has 4 lines of symmetry
Has rotational symmetry of order 4

Diagram:

D————————C
|	|
|	|
|	|
A————————B

All sides equal: AB = BC = CD = DA All angles = 90°
Formulas:

Perimeter: P = 4s (where s = side length)
Area: A = s²
Diagonal: d = s√2
Example 1: Square with side 5 cm Perimeter = 4 × 5 = 20 cm
Area = 5² = 25 cm² Diagonal = 5√2 ≈ 7.07 cm
Real-Life Examples:

Chess board squares
Floor tiles
Window panes
Checkerboard
Book covers (sometimes)

RECTANGLE

Definition: A rectangle is a quadrilateral with opposite sides equal and all angles equal to 90°.

Properties:

Opposite sides are equal (AB = CD, BC = DA)
All angles are 90°
Opposite sides are parallel
Diagonals are equal in length
Diagonals bisect each other (but not at 90°)
Has 2 lines of symmetry (vertical and horizontal)
Has rotational symmetry of order 2

Diagram:

D—————————————C
|	|
|	|
A—————————————B

Length: AB = DC (longer sides) Width: BC = AD (shorter sides) All angles = 90°
Formulas:

Perimeter: P = 2(l + w) or P = 2l + 2w
Area: A = l × w
Diagonal: d = √(l² + w²)
Example 2: Rectangle with length 8 cm and width 5 cm Perimeter = 2(8 + 5) = 2(13) = 26 cm
Area = 8 × 5 = 40 cm²
Diagonal = √(8² + 5²) = √(64 + 25) = √89 ≈ 9.43 cm

Real-Life Examples:

Books
Doors
Tables
Paper (A4, etc.)
Computer screens
Football pitch

Difference from Square:

Rectangle: Only opposite sides equal
Square: All sides equal (special type of rectangle)

PARALLELOGRAM

Definition: A parallelogram is a quadrilateral with opposite sides parallel and equal.

Properties:

Opposite sides are parallel (AB ∥ DC, AD ∥ BC)
Opposite sides are equal (AB = DC, AD = BC)
Opposite angles are equal (∠A = ∠C, ∠B = ∠D)
Adjacent angles are supplementary (sum to 180°)
Diagonals bisect each other (but not equal, not at 90°)
No lines of symmetry (unless it's a rectangle)
Has rotational symmetry of order 2

Diagram:

D————————C
/	/
/	/
/	/
A————————B

AB ∥ DC (parallel) AD ∥ BC (parallel) AB = DC, AD = BC
Formulas:

Perimeter: P = 2(a + b) where a and b are adjacent sides
Area: A = base × height (perpendicular height)
Area: A = b × h

Example 3: Parallelogram with base 10 cm, side 6 cm, height 4 cm

Perimeter = 2(10 + 6) = 32 cm
Area = base × height = 10 × 4 = 40 cm²

Real-Life Examples:

Slanted roof supports
Conveyor belt systems
Some bridge designs
Erasers (some shapes)
Tiles (some patterns)

Key Point: Height is PERPENDICULAR distance between parallel sides, not the slant side length.

RHOMBUS

Definition: A rhombus is a parallelogram with all sides equal.

Properties:

All sides are equal (AB = BC = CD = DA)
Opposite sides are parallel
Opposite angles are equal
Diagonals bisect each other at 90° (right angles)
Diagonals bisect the angles
Has 2 lines of symmetry (along diagonals)
Has rotational symmetry of order 2
Angles are NOT necessarily 90° (if they are, it's a square)

Diagram:

D
/|\
/ | \
/ | \ A———+———C
\ | /
\ | /
\|/ B

All sides equal: AB = BC = CD = DA Diagonals AC and BD bisect at 90°
Formulas:

Perimeter: P = 4s (where s = side length)
Area: A = (d₁ × d₂)/2 (where d₁, d₂ are diagonals)
Area: A = base × height

Example 4: Rhombus with side 6 cm, diagonals 8 cm and 10 cm

Perimeter = 4 × 6 = 24 cm
Area = (8 × 10)/2 = 80/2 = 40 cm²

Real-Life Examples:

Diamond shape (hence name "diamond")
Kite shapes (some)
Road signs (some)
Jewelry designs
Pattern designs

Difference from Square:

Rhombus: Angles not necessarily 90°
Square: All angles are 90° (special type of rhombus)

Difference from Parallelogram:

Rhombus: All sides equal
Parallelogram: Only opposite sides equal

KITE

Definition: A kite is a quadrilateral with two pairs of adjacent sides equal.

Properties:

Two pairs of adjacent sides are equal (AB = AD, CB = CD)
One pair of opposite angles are equal (the angles between unequal sides)
Diagonals intersect at 90° (perpendicular)
One diagonal bisects the other
One diagonal is the axis of symmetry
Has 1 line of symmetry
No rotational symmetry (order 1)

Diagram:

D
/|\
/ | \
/ | \
A | C
\ | /
\ | /
\|/ B

AD = AB (one pair) CD = CB (another pair)
Diagonal DB bisects AC at 90°

Formulas:

Perimeter: P = 2(a + b) where a and b are the unequal adjacent sides
Area: A = (d₁ × d₂)/2 (where d₁, d₂ are diagonals)
Example 5: Kite with sides 5 cm and 7 cm (each pair), diagonals 8 cm and 6 cm Perimeter = 2(5 + 7) = 2(12) = 24 cm
Area = (8 × 6)/2 = 48/2 = 24 cm²

Real-Life Examples:

Flying kites
Arrowheads
Some gemstone cuts
Decorative designs
Some logos

COMPARISON TABLE

PropertySquareRectangleParallelogramRhombusKiteAll sides equalYesNoNoYesNoOpposite sides equalYesYesYesYesNoAdjacent sides equalYesNoNoYesYes (2 pairs)All angles 90°YesYesNoNoNoPropertySquareRectangleParallelogramRhombusKiteOpposite angles equalYesYesYesYesOne pairOpposite sides parallelYesYesYesYesNoDiagonals equalYesYesNoNoNoDiagonals bisect each otherYesYesYesYesOne bisects otherDiagonals perpendicularYesNoNoYesYesLines of symmetry42021Rotational symmetry order42221

IDENTIFYING SHAPES IN THE ENVIRONMENT

Classroom Objects:

Floor tiles - Square/Rectangle
Whiteboard - Rectangle
Window panes - Square/Rectangle
Book covers - Rectangle
Desk tops - Rectangle

Home Objects:

Doors - Rectangle
Tiles - Square
Mirrors - Square/Rectangle
Beds - Rectangle
TVs/Monitors - Rectangle

Outdoor Objects:

Football pitch - Rectangle
Building facades - Rectangle
Road signs - Various shapes
Kites - Kite shape
Windows - Square/Rectangle

CALCULATING PERIMETER AND AREA

Example 6: Square courtyard with side 15 m

a) Perimeter = 4 × 15 = 60 m

b) Area = 15² = 225 m²

Cost to fence at ₦500/m:
= 60 × 500 = ₦30,000

Cost to tile at ₦2,000/m²:
= 225 × 2,000 = ₦450,000

Example 7: Rectangular field 50 m by 30 m

a) Perimeter = 2(50 + 30) = 160 m

b) Area = 50 × 30 = 1,500 m²

c) Diagonal = √(50² + 30²)
= √(2,500 + 900)
= √3,400
≈ 58.3 m
Example 8: Rhombus-shaped garden with diagonals 12 m and 16 m a) Area = (12 × 16)/2
= 192/2
= 96 m²
b) If each side is 10 m:
Perimeter = 4 × 10 = 40 m

Example 9: Parallelogram-shaped plot with base 20 m, side 15 m, height 12 m

Perimeter = 2(20 + 15) = 70 m

Area = base × height
= 20 × 12
= 240 m²

Example 10: Kite-shaped logo with sides 8 cm and 10 cm, diagonals 12 cm and 14 cm

a) Perimeter = 2(8 + 10) = 36 cm

b) Area = (12 × 14)/2 = 84 cm²

PROBLEM SOLVING WITH SHAPES

Example 11: A rectangular garden is 24 m long and 18 m wide. A path 2 m wide runs around the inside. Find the area of the path.
Solution:
Outer rectangle: 24 m × 18 m Area = 24 × 18 = 432 m²

Inner rectangle (minus path):
Length = 24 - 2(2) = 20 m
Width = 18 - 2(2) = 14 m
Area = 20 × 14 = 280 m²

Path area = 432 - 280 = 152 m²

Example 12: A square field has perimeter 120 m. Find: a) Side length b) Area c) Diagonal
Solution:
Perimeter = 4s 120 = 4s
s = 30 m

Area = s²
= 30²
= 900 m²

Diagonal = s√2
= 30√2
≈ 42.43 m

WEEK 10: SCALE DRAWING OF LENGTH AND DISTANCES

TOPIC: Meaning and Purpose of Scale Drawing; Measurement and Representation on Paper

CONTENT:
INTRODUCTION TO SCALE DRAWING

Definition: A scale drawing is a reduced or enlarged representation of an actual object or distance that maintains accurate proportions.
Why Use Scale Drawings?

To represent large objects/distances on paper (buildings, maps, etc.)
To represent small objects in larger form (cells, insects, etc.)
To maintain accurate proportions
To communicate designs and plans
To measure actual distances from drawings

Real-Life Applications:

Maps and atlases
Architectural plans
Engineering blueprints
Model cars, planes, buildings
Garden designs
Room layouts

UNDERSTANDING SCALE

Definition of Scale: A scale is the ratio of the length on a drawing to the actual length of the object.
Format: Scale is written as:

Ratio form: 1:100 (read as "1 to 100")
Fraction form: 1/100
Statement form: "1 cm represents 100 cm" or "1 cm = 1 m"

Meaning:

Scale 1:100 means:
1 cm on drawing = 100 cm in reality
1 unit on drawing = 100 units in reality

TYPES OF SCALES

Reduction Scale (Most common)

Drawing is smaller than actual object
Scale ratio: 1:n where n > 1
Examples: 1:10, 1:50, 1:100, 1:1000

Example: 1:50 scale

1 cm on paper = 50 cm in reality

Enlargement Scale (Less common)

Drawing is larger than actual object
Scale ratio: n:1 where n > 1
Examples: 10:1, 100:1, 1000:1
Used for: microscopic objects, small insects, etc.

Example: 10:1 scale

10 cm on paper = 1 cm in reality

Full Scale (1:1)

Drawing is same size as object
Rarely used except for small objects

COMMON SCALES USED

Building Plans:

1:50 (1 cm = 50 cm = 0.5 m)
1:100 (1 cm = 100 cm = 1 m)
1:200 (1 cm = 200 cm = 2 m)

Maps:

1:10,000 (1 cm = 10,000 cm = 100 m)
1:50,000 (1 cm = 50,000 cm = 500 m = 0.5 km)
1:100,000 (1 cm = 100,000 cm = 1,000 m = 1 km)
1:1,000,000 (1 cm = 10 km)

Models:

Model cars: 1:24, 1:32, 1:64
Model planes: 1:72, 1:48
Architectural models: 1:200, 1:500

CALCULATING ACTUAL LENGTH FROM SCALE DRAWING

Formula: Actual Length = Drawing Length × Scale Factor
Example 1: Scale 1:100 A line measures 5 cm on drawing. Find actual length. Scale 1:100 means 1 cm = 100 cm
Actual length = 5 × 100 = 500 cm = 5 m
Example 2: Scale 1:50 Drawing shows 8 cm. Find actual length. 1 cm = 50 cm
Actual length = 8 × 50 = 400 cm = 4 m
Example 3: Scale 1:200 On a plan, a room measures 3 cm by 2.5 cm. Find actual dimensions.

Length: 3 × 200 = 600 cm = 6 m
Width: 2.5 × 200 = 500 cm = 5 m Actual room: 6 m by 5 m
Example 4: Map scale 1:10,000 Distance on map is 4.5 cm. Find actual distance.

Actual distance = 4.5 × 10,000 cm
= 45,000 cm
= 450 m
= 0.45 km
Example 5: Map scale 1:50,000 Two towns are 7 cm apart on map. Find actual distance. Actual distance = 7 × 50,000 cm
= 350,000 cm
= 3,500 m
= 3.5 km

CALCULATING DRAWING LENGTH FROM ACTUAL LENGTH

Formula: Drawing Length = Actual Length ÷ Scale Factor Example 6: Scale 1:100 Actual length is 12 m. Find drawing length.
Convert to same units: 12 m = 1,200 cm Drawing length = 1,200 ÷ 100 = 12 cm
Example 7: Scale 1:50 A wall is 8 m long. How long on drawing?

8 m = 800 cm
Drawing length = 800 ÷ 50 = 16 cm

Example 8: Scale 1:200 Garden is 30 m by 20 m. Find dimensions on plan.
Length: 3,000 cm ÷ 200 = 15 cm
Width: 2,000 cm ÷ 200 = 10 cm On plan: 15 cm by 10 cm
Example 9: Map scale 1:25,000 Actual distance is 5 km. Find map distance.

5 km = 5,000 m = 500,000 cm
Map distance = 500,000 ÷ 25,000 = 20 cm
Example 10: Scale 1:1000 Distance between two points is 750 m. Find drawing distance. 750 m = 75,000 cm
Drawing distance = 75,000 ÷ 1,000 = 75 cm

FINDING THE SCALE

Formula: Scale = Drawing Length : Actual Length (Both must be in same units)
Example 11: Drawing length is 6 cm, actual length is 300 cm. Find scale. Scale = 6 : 300
= 1 : 50  (divide both by 6)
Scale is 1:50
Example 12: On a plan, 4 cm represents 8 m. Find scale. Convert to same units: 8 m = 800 cm
Scale = 4 : 800
= 1 : 200  (divide both by 4)
Scale is 1:200
Example 13: A map shows 5 cm for actual distance of 2.5 km. Find scale. 2.5 km = 2,500 m = 250,000 cm
Scale = 5 : 250,000
= 1 : 50,000  (divide both by 5)
Scale is 1:50,000
Example 14: Drawing: 8 cm, Actual: 4 m. Find scale. 4 m = 400 cm
Scale = 8 : 400
= 1 : 50
Scale is 1:50

SCALE DRAWINGS - PRACTICAL EXAMPLES

Example 15: Room Plan (Scale 1:50) Given: Actual room 7 m long, 5 m wide Step 1: Calculate drawing dimensions
Length: 700 cm ÷ 50 = 14 cm
Width: 500 cm ÷ 50 = 10 cm

Step 2: Draw rectangle 14 cm × 10 cm
Step 3: Add features (door 1 m wide, window 1.5 m) Door: 100 cm ÷ 50 = 2 cm
Window: 150 cm ÷ 50 = 3 cm

Step 4: Label: "Scale 1:50" and dimensions

Example 16: Garden Design (Scale 1:100)

Actual garden: 20 m by 15 m with:

Path 2 m wide along one side
Flower bed 4 m × 3 m
Lawn area (remaining)

Drawing dimensions:

Garden: 2,000 cm ÷ 100 = 20 cm by 15 cm
Path: 200 cm ÷ 100 = 2 cm wide Flower bed: 4 cm × 3 cm
Draw and label all sections

Example 17: Classroom Floor Plan (Scale 1:50)

Actual dimensions:

Room: 10 m × 8 m
Teacher's desk: 1.5 m × 1 m
Student desks: 1.2 m × 0.6 m (6 desks)
Door: 1 m wide
Windows: 2 m wide (2 windows)

Calculate drawing dimensions:

Room: 20 cm × 16 cm Teacher's desk: 3 cm × 2 cm Student desks: 2.4 cm × 1.2 cm Door: 2 cm
Windows: 4 cm each

AREA CALCULATIONS FROM SCALE DRAWINGS

Key Concept: Area scale is the square of length scale.

If scale is 1:n, then area scale is 1:n²
Example 18: Scale 1:100 Rectangle on drawing: 5 cm × 4 cm Find actual area.
Method 1: Find actual dimensions first Actual length = 5 × 100 = 500 cm = 5 m
Actual width = 4 × 100 = 400 cm = 4 m
Actual area = 5 × 4 = 20 m²

Method 2: Use area scale

Drawing area = 5 × 4 = 20 cm² Area scale = 1:100² = 1:10,000
Actual area = 20 × 10,000 cm² = 200,000 cm² = 20 m²
Example 19: Scale 1:50 Drawing shows room with area 12 cm². Find actual area. Area scale = 1:50² = 1:2,500
Actual area = 12 × 2,500 = 30,000 cm² = 3 m²
Example 20: Scale 1:200 Field on plan measures 8 cm × 6 cm. Find actual area. Drawing area = 48 cm²
Area scale = 1:40,000
Actual area = 48 × 40,000 cm² = 1,920,000 cm² = 192 m²

OR:
Actual length = 8 × 200 = 1,600 cm = 16 m
Actual width = 6 × 200 = 1,200 cm = 12 m
Actual area = 16 × 12 = 192 m²

MAP READING AND DISTANCE CALCULATIONS

Example 21: Map scale 1:50,000 Two villages are 8 cm apart on map.
Actual distance:
8 × 50,000 = 400,000 cm = 4 km

If traveling at 60 km/h, time taken: Time = 4 ÷ 60 = 0.067 hours = 4 minutes
Example 22: Map scale 1:25,000 A road measures 12 cm on map.
Actual length:
12 × 25,000 = 300,000 cm = 3 km

If walking at 5 km/h:
Time = 3 ÷ 5 = 0.6 hours = 36 minutes

Example 23: Map scale 1:100,000
Town A to Town B is 15 km. How far on map? 15 km = 1,500,000 cm
Map distance = 1,500,000 ÷ 100,000 = 15 cm

CREATING SCALE DRAWINGS

Steps to Create a Scale Drawing:

Choose appropriate scale

Consider paper size
Consider actual dimensions
o Common scales: 1:50, 1:100, 1:200
Calculate drawing dimensions

Convert actual to same units
Divide by scale factor
Draw using ruler and pencil

Use sharp pencil
Draw lightly first
Check measurements
Add details and labels

Mark dimensions
Show scale
Add title
Make neat and clear

Use straight lines
Label clearly
Show orientation (N, S, E, W if needed)

Example 24: Draw a scale plan of rectangular plot 40 m × 30 m at scale 1:500

Step 1: Choose scale (given): 1:500
Step 2: Calculate dimensions Length: 4,000 cm ÷ 500 = 8 cm
Width: 3,000 cm ÷ 500 = 6 cm

Step 3: Draw rectangle 8 cm × 6 cm

Step 4: Label

Mark "40 m" and "30 m"
Write "Scale 1:500"
Add title "Plot Plan"

COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID
Not converting to same units

Wrong: Scale 1:100, actual 5 m
Drawing = 5 ÷ 100 = 0.05 m ✗

Right: Scale 1:100, actual 5 m = 500 cm
Drawing = 500 ÷ 100 = 5 cm ✓

Confusing which to divide/multiply

Actual to drawing: DIVIDE by scale
Drawing to actual: MULTIPLY by scale
Forgetting to simplify scale

Scale 6:300 should be simplified to 1:50

Wrong area scale

If length scale is 1:100
Area scale is 1:10,000 (not 1:100)

PRACTICAL APPLICATIONS

Application 1: Building Plans

An architect draws a house plan at 1:100.

Living room: 6 m × 5 m → 6 cm × 5 cm on plan
Bedroom: 4 m × 3.5 m → 4 cm × 3.5 cm on plan
Kitchen: 3 m × 2.5 m → 3 cm × 2.5 cm on plan

Application 2: Maps

A town map at 1:10,000 shows:
School to hospital: 5 cm on map = 500 m actual
Hospital to market: 3.5 cm = 350 m actual
Total journey: 850 m Application 3: Models Model car at scale 1:32
Actual car length: 4.8 m
Model length: 480 cm ÷ 32 = 15 cm

WEEK 11: QUANTITATIVE APTITUDE

TOPIC: Meaning of Quantitative Aptitude; Reasons for Studying It; Solving Problems Related to Plane Shapes/Figures and Scale
CONTENT:
INTRODUCTION TO QUANTITATIVE APTITUDE

Definition: Quantitative aptitude is the ability to handle numerical and mathematical problems efficiently and accurately. It involves reasoning with numbers, solving mathematical problems, and applying mathematical concepts to real-life situations.
Components:

Numerical ability - working with numbers
Problem-solving skills - finding solutions
Logical reasoning - thinking systematically
Speed and accuracy - solving quickly and correctly
Application skills - using math in real situations

IMPORTANCE OF QUANTITATIVE APTITUDE

Why Study Quantitative Aptitude?

Academic Success

Essential for mathematics exams
Needed for science subjects
Important for competitive exams
Foundation for higher studies
Career Development

Required in many professions
Important for job interviews
Needed for aptitude tests
Essential for business
Daily Life Applications

Shopping and budgeting
Time management
Measurement and estimation
Financial planning
Mental Development

Improves logical thinking
Enhances problem-solving skills
Develops analytical ability
Sharpens mental calculation
Competitive Advantage

Better exam performance
Success in entrance tests
Improved job prospects
Business decision-making

TYPES OF QUANTITATIVE REASONING PROBLEMS

Number Problems 2. Ratio and Proportion 3. Percentage Applications 4. Profit and Loss
5. Simple and Compound Interest 6. Time and Work 7. Time, Speed, and Distance 8. Averages 9. Mensuration (Areas and Volumes) 10. Data Interpretation

QUANTITATIVE REASONING WITH PLANE SHAPES

Type 1: Perimeter Problems

Example 1: A rectangular field has length 50 m and perimeter 180 m. Find: a) Width b) Area c) Cost to fence at ₦350 per meter
Solution:
Perimeter = 2(l + w) 180 = 2(50 + w)
180 = 100 + 2w
80 = 2w
w = 40 m

Area = l × w
= 50 × 40
= 2,000 m²

Cost = Perimeter × Rate
= 180 × 350
= ₦63,000
Example 2: A square field has perimeter 120 m. Find: a) Side length b) Area c) Diagonal length Solution:
Perimeter = 4s 120 = 4s
s = 30 m

Area = s²
= 30²
= 900 m²

Diagonal = s√2
= 30√2
≈ 42.43 m

Type 2: Area Problems

Example 3: A rectangle has area 240 m² and length 20 m. Find: a) Width b) Perimeter c) Cost to tile at ₦2,500/m²
Solution:
Area = l × w 240 = 20 × w w = 12 m

Perimeter = 2(20 + 12)
= 64 m

c) Cost = 240 × 2,500
= ₦600,000

Example 4: A rhombus has diagonals 24 cm and 18 cm. Find: a) Area b) If each side is 15 cm, find perimeter
Solution:
Area = (d₁ × d₂)/2
= (24 × 18)/2
= 216 cm²

Perimeter = 4 × 15
= 60 cm

Type 3: Comparison Problems

Example 5: Square A has side 10 cm. Square B has side 15 cm. a) Find area of each b) How much larger is B than A? c) Find ratio of areas
Solution:
Area A = 10² = 100 cm² Area B = 15² = 225 cm²

Difference = 225 - 100 = 125 cm² B is 125 cm² larger

c) Ratio = 100:225 = 4:9

Example 6: Rectangle P: 12 cm × 8 cm Rectangle Q: 15 cm × 10 cm Which has greater perimeter? By how much?
Solution:
Perimeter P = 2(12 + 8) = 40 cm
Perimeter Q = 2(15 + 10) = 50 cm

Q has greater perimeter by 50 - 40 = 10 cm

Type 4: Cost Calculation Problems

Example 7: A rectangular plot 40 m × 30 m needs:

Fencing at ₦450 per meter
Tiling at ₦3,000 per m² Find total cost.
Solution:
Perimeter = 2(40 + 30) = 140 m
Fencing cost = 140 × 450 = ₦63,000

Area = 40 × 30 = 1,200 m²
Tiling cost = 1,200 × 3,000 = ₦3,600,000

Total cost = 63,000 + 3,600,000 = ₦3,663,000

Example 8: A square garden of side 20 m has:

Path 2 m wide around inside
Grass costs ₦1,500/m² for inner area
Paving costs ₦2,500/m² for path Find total cost.
Solution:
Outer area = 20² = 400 m²
Inner garden side = 20 - 2(2) = 16 m Inner area = 16² = 256 m²
Path area = 400 - 256 = 144 m²

Grass cost = 256 × 1,500 = ₦384,000 Path cost = 144 × 2,500 = ₦360,000
Total = ₦744,000

QUANTITATIVE REASONING WITH SCALE

Type 1: Scale Conversion Problems Example 9: A building plan uses scale 1:100.
Drawing dimensions: 15 cm × 12 cm
Find: a) Actual dimensions b) Actual area c) Cost to build at ₦50,000/m²
Solution:
a) Length = 15 × 100 = 1,500 cm = 15 m
Width = 12 × 100 = 1,200 cm = 12 m

b) Area = 15 × 12 = 180 m²

c) Cost = 180 × 50,000 = ₦9,000,000

Example 10: Map scale 1:50,000

School to library: 8 cm on map
Library to hospital: 6 cm on map
Find total actual distance in km Solution:
School to library = 8 × 50,000 = 400,000 cm = 4 km
Library to hospital = 6 × 50,000 = 300,000 cm = 3 km
Total distance = 4 + 3 = 7 km

Type 2: Time and Distance with Scale Example 11: Map scale 1:25,000
Two towns are 10 cm apart on map
Traveling at 50 km/h, find time taken Solution:
Actual distance = 10 × 25,000 = 250,000 cm = 2.5 km
Time = Distance ÷ Speed
= 2.5 ÷ 50
= 0.05 hours
= 3 minutes

Example 12: Scale 1:100,000
Runner completes route in 30 minutes at 12 km/h
How long is route on map? Solution:
Time = 30 min = 0.5 hours
Actual distance = Speed × Time
= 12 × 0.5
= 6 km = 600,000 cm

Map distance = 600,000 ÷ 100,000 = 6 cm

COMBINED REASONING PROBLEMS

Example 13: A rectangular field 60 m × 40 m has:

Square pond of side 10 m in one corner
Path 3 m wide along all sides inside
Remaining area for crops
Find: a) Area for crops b) If yield is 5 kg/m², total yield c) Selling at ₦200/kg, total revenue Solution:
a) Total area = 60 × 40 = 2,400 m² Pond area = 10² = 100 m²

Inner dimensions (minus path): 54 m × 34 m = 1,836 m²

Crop area = 1,836 - 100 = 1,736 m²

b) Yield = 1,736 × 5 = 8,680 kg

c) Revenue = 8,680 × 200 = ₦1,736,000

Example 14: A town map at scale 1:20,000 shows:

Park: rectangle 6 cm × 4 cm
School: square 3 cm side
Road connecting them: 5 cm
Find: a) Actual areas b) Actual road length c) Walking time at 4 km/h Solution:
a) Park:
Actual: 120 m × 80 m = 9,600 m²

School:
Actual: 60 m × 60 m = 3,600 m²

b) Road = 5 × 20,000 = 100,000 cm = 1 km

c) Time = 1 ÷ 4 = 0.25 hours = 15 minutes

PERCENTAGE PROBLEMS WITH SHAPES

Example 15: A rectangular plot has area 1,500 m².

40% for building
25% for garden
20% for parking
Remaining for lawn
Find area of each section. Solution:
Building = 40% of 1,500 = 600 m²
Garden = 25% of 1,500 = 375 m²
Parking = 20% of 1,500 = 300 m²
Lawn = 1,500 - (600 + 375 + 300) = 225 m²

OR: Lawn = 15% of 1,500 = 225 m²

Example 16: A square field's side is increased by 20%. Find percentage increase in: a) Perimeter
b) Area

Solution:
Let original side = s New side = 1.2s

Original perimeter = 4s
New perimeter = 4(1.2s) = 4.8s Increase = 4.8s - 4s = 0.8s
% increase = (0.8s/4s) × 100% = 20%

Original area = s²
New area = (1.2s)² = 1.44s² Increase = 1.44s² - s² = 0.44s²
% increase = (0.44s²/s²) × 100% = 44%

RATIO AND PROPORTION PROBLEMS

Example 17: Two rectangles have areas in ratio 3:5. If smaller has area 90 cm², find: a) Area of larger b) If both have same width 6 cm, find lengths
Solution:
Let areas be 3x and 5x 3x = 90
x = 30
Larger area = 5 × 30 = 150 cm²

Smaller: Area = l × w 90 = l × 6
l = 15 cm

Larger: 150 = l × 6 l = 25 cm
Example 18: Sides of a rectangle are in ratio 4:3. If perimeter is 140 cm, find: a) Dimensions b) Area
Solution:
a) Let sides be 4x and 3x Perimeter = 2(4x + 3x) = 14x 14x = 140
x = 10

Length = 4 × 10 = 40 cm
Width = 3 × 10 = 30 cm

b) Area = 40 × 30 = 1,200 cm²

PRACTICAL REASONING STRATEGIES

Strategy 1: Understand the Problem

Read carefully
Identify what is given
Identify what is asked
Draw diagrams if helpful

Strategy 2: Choose the Right Formula
Perimeter formulas
Area formulas
Scale formulas
Cost = Quantity × Rate

Strategy 3: Work Systematically

Show all steps
Convert units if needed
Check calculations
Verify answer makes sense

Strategy 4: Use Estimation

Round numbers for quick check
Estimate before calculating
Check if answer is reasonable

Strategy 5: Multiple Approaches

Try different methods
Use what works best
Check answer different way

J. COMMON QUESTION PATTERNS

Pattern 1: "Find and Calculate"

Find missing dimension
Then calculate another value

Pattern 2: "Comparison"

Compare two shapes
Find difference or ratio

Pattern 3: "Cost Calculation"

Find area/perimeter
Multiply by rate

Pattern 4: "Percentage Change"

Find new value after % increase/decrease
Calculate effect

Pattern 5: "Word to Math"

Translate word problem
Set up equation
Solve

JSS2 MATHEMATICS LESSON NOTES THIRD TERM

WEEK 1: REVISION OF SECOND TERM'S EXAMINATION

TOPIC: Representation of Real-Life Situations on a Graph and Reasons

CONTENT:
REVIEW OF GRAPHING CONCEPTS

Key Points from Second Term:

Cartesian plane and coordinates
Linear equations in two variables
Graphing linear equations
Reading and interpreting graphs
Real-life applications

WHY USE GRAPHS FOR REAL-LIFE SITUATIONS?

Advantages of Graphs:

Visual Representation

Easy to understand at a glance
Shows patterns and trends clearly
Better than long tables or text
Quick Comparison

Compare different data sets easily
See relationships between variables
Identify maximum and minimum values
Prediction and Estimation
Extend lines to predict future values
Interpolate between known points
Make informed decisions
Communication

Universal language
Effective in presentations
Clear to wide audience
Problem Solving

Visualize solutions
Identify break-even points
Optimize decisions

TYPES OF REAL-LIFE GRAPHS

Distance-Time Graphs

Shows relationship between distance traveled and time taken.
Example 1: A car travels from home to market Distance (km)
|
50 |	——————
40 |	/	\
30 |	/	\
20 |	/	\
10 |	/	\
|_	/	\
——————O————————————————————— Time (hours) 0 1 2 3 4 5 6
Interpretation:
0-2 hrs: Traveling to market (30 km away) 2-4 hrs: At market (stationary - flat line)
hrs: Returning home

Key Features:

Slope = Speed (steeper = faster)
Horizontal line = Stationary (not moving)
Rising line = Going away
Falling line = Returning Example 2: Journey Analysis
Distance from home vs Time:
First hour: Travels 40 km (speed = 40 km/h)
Next 2 hours: Stationary
Last 2 hours: Returns 40 km (speed = 20 km/h)

Temperature-Time Graphs

Shows how temperature changes over time.
Example 3: Daily Temperature Temperature (°C)

6am 9am 12pm 3pm 6pm

Coolest at dawn
Rises through morning
Peaks at midday
Falls in evening

Cost-Quantity Graphs

Shows relationship between cost and quantity purchased.

Example 4: Mobile Phone Bills Plan: ₦500 monthly + ₦5 per minute Equation: C = 5m + 500

Profit-Loss Graphs Example 5: Business Analysis

Equations:

Revenue: R = 500n
Cost: C = 300n + 200,000

Break-even point: Where Revenue = Cost 500n = 300n + 200,000
200n = 200,000
n = 1,000 units

Identify Variables

CREATING GRAPHS FROM REAL-LIFE DATA

Steps:

Identify variables
Take measurements
Record in table

Suit the data range

PRACTICAL APPLICATIONS

Application 1: Business Decision

A company compares two pricing strategies by graphing revenue vs units sold. Graph helps choose better strategy.
Application 2: Health Monitoring
Patient's temperature graphed over days. Helps doctor see improvement or worsening.

Application 3: Academic Performance

Student's test scores graphed over terms. Shows progress, identifies weak areas.

Application 4: Budget Planning

Family expenses graphed monthly. Helps identify spending patterns, areas to save.

Application 5: Sports Performance

Athlete's running time graphed over training weeks. Shows improvement, motivates training.

COMMON GRAPH MISTAKES TO AVOID
Wrong Scale

Too cramped or too spread out
Inconsistent intervals
Unlabeled Axes

Readers can't understand graph
No units shown
Inaccurate Plotting

Points in wrong positions
Careless measurement
Wrong Line Type

Straight line through scattered data
Connecting unrelated points
Missing Title

Purpose unclear
Context missing

WEEK 2: ANGLES AND POLYGONS

TOPIC: Definition of Angles; Construction of Angles; Polygons and Interior Angles

CONTENT:
ANGLES - DEFINITION AND TYPES

Definition: An angle is the amount of turn between two straight lines that meet at a point (vertex).
Parts of an Angle:

Ray B (Arm)
/
/
/	Ray A (Arm) O (Vertex)

Angle AOB or ∠AOB

Measuring Angles:

Unit: Degrees (°)
Tool: Protractor
Full turn: 360°
Straight line: 180°
Right angle: 90°

TYPES OF ANGLES

Acute Angle

\
\
\
0° < angle < 90°

Examples: 30°, 45°, 60°, 75°

Right Angle

|
|
|_	 Angle = 90°
Obtuse Angle

/
/
/
90° < angle < 180°

Examples: 105°, 120°, 135°, 150°

Straight Angle

———————
Angle = 180°

Reflex Angle

(larger turn)
180° < angle < 360°

Examples: 210°, 270°, 315°

Complete/Full Angle

(complete turn) Angle = 360°

ANGLE RELATIONSHIPS

Complementary Angles Two angles that add up to 90°

Example 1:

30° and 60° are complementary
30° + 60° = 90°

Example 2: Find complement of 35°

Complement = 90° - 35° = 55°

Supplementary Angles Two angles that add up to 180°

Example 3:

110° and 70° are supplementary
110° + 70° = 180°

Example 4: Find supplement of 125°

Supplement = 180° - 125° = 55°
Vertically Opposite Angles When two lines cross, opposite angles are equal a° \ / b°
X
c° / \ d°

a° = c° (opposite) b° = d° (opposite)
a° + b° = 180° (supplementary)

Adjacent Angles Angles that share a common arm and vertex

/
/b°
/	a°

a° and b° are adjacent

CONSTRUCTING ANGLES USING PROTRACTOR AND COMPASS

Using Protractor (Direct Method): Example 5: Construct 75°
Steps:

Draw a base line AB
Place protractor center on A
Mark 75° from base line
Join A to mark
Label angle

Using Compass and Ruler (Construction Method): Basic Angles You Can Construct:
60° (equilateral triangle)
90° (perpendicular)
120° (supplement of 60°)
30° (bisect 60°)
45° (bisect 90°)
Others by combination

Constructing 60°:

Steps:
Draw line AB
With A as center, draw arc
With same radius from arc intersection, draw another arc
Join A to intersection point Result: ∠ = 60°
Constructing 90° (Perpendicular):

Steps:
Draw line AB
From point A, draw arcs above and below line
From both intersections, draw arcs to intersect
Join A to intersection point Result: ∠ = 90°
Constructing 30°:

Construct 60°, then bisect it Result: 30°
Constructing 45°:

Construct 90°, then bisect it Result: 45°

Constructing 75°:

Method 1: 60° + 15°

Construct 60°
Bisect 30° to get 15°
Add 60° + 15° = 75°

Method 2: 90° - 15°

Construct 90°
Construct 30° then bisect for 15°
Subtract 15° from 90°

Constructing 105°:

Method: 90° + 15° OR 60° + 45°

Construct 90° and 15°, combine them OR
Construct 60° and 45°, combine them
Result: 105°

Constructing 120°:

Method: Supplement of 60°

Construct 60° on opposite side of 180° line OR
Construct 60° + 60°
Result: 120°

Constructing 135°:

Method: 90° + 45°

Construct 90°
Construct 45° Combine them Result: 135°

POLYGONS

Definition: A polygon is a closed plane figure made up of three or more straight line segments.

Parts of a Polygon:

Sides: The straight line segments
Vertices: Points where sides meet
Interior angles: Angles inside the polygon
Exterior angles: Angles outside the polygon
Diagonals: Lines joining non-adjacent vertices

Types of Polygons by Number of Sides:

NameSidesExampleTriangle3△Quadrilateral4▭Pentagon5⬠Hexagon6⬡Heptagon7Octagon8⯃Nonagon9Decagon10Dodecagon12n-gonn

Types of Polygons by Regularity:

Regular Polygon:

All sides equal
All angles equal
Examples: Equilateral triangle, square, regular pentagon

Irregular Polygon:

Sides and/or angles not all equal
Examples: Rectangle, scalene triangle
Convex Polygon:

All interior angles < 180°
All vertices point outward

Concave Polygon:

At least one interior angle > 180°
At least one vertex points inward

SUM OF INTERIOR ANGLES

Formula: Sum of interior angles = (n - 2) × 180° OR Sum = (2n - 4) right angles (since 1 right angle = 90°)
Where n = number of sides

Derivation: A polygon with n sides can be divided into (n - 2) triangles from one vertex. Each triangle has angle sum = 180° Therefore, total = (n - 2) × 180°

Example 6: Triangle (n = 3)

Sum = (3 - 2) × 180°
= 1 × 180°
= 180°

Example 7: Quadrilateral (n = 4)

Sum = (4 - 2) × 180°
= 2 × 180°
= 360°

Example 8: Pentagon (n = 5)
Sum = (5 - 2) × 180°
= 3 × 180°
= 540°

Example 9: Hexagon (n = 6)

Sum = (6 - 2) × 180°
= 4 × 180°
= 720°

Example 10: Octagon (n = 8)

Sum = (8 - 2) × 180°
= 6 × 180°
= 1,080°

EACH INTERIOR ANGLE OF REGULAR POLYGON

Formula: Each interior angle = [(n - 2) × 180°] / n

OR

Each interior angle = [(2n - 4) × 90°] / n
Example 11: Each angle in regular pentagon n = 5
Each angle = [(5 - 2) × 180°] / 5
= [3 × 180°] / 5
= 540° / 5
= 108°
Example 12: Each angle in regular hexagon n = 6
Each angle = [(6 - 2) × 180°] / 6
= 720° / 6
= 120°
Example 13: Each angle in regular octagon n = 8
Each angle = [(8 - 2) × 180°] / 8
= 1,080° / 8
= 135°

EXTERIOR ANGLES

Properties:

Exterior angle + Adjacent interior angle = 180°
Sum of all exterior angles = 360° (for any convex polygon)

For Regular Polygon: Each exterior angle = 360° / n
Example 14: Each exterior angle of regular pentagon n = 5
Each exterior angle = 360° / 5 = 72°

Check: Interior + Exterior = 180° 108° + 72° = 180° ✓
Example 15: Each exterior angle of regular hexagon Each exterior angle = 360° / 6 = 60°

I. FINDING NUMBER OF SIDES

If given each interior angle:
Example 16: Each interior angle is 140°. Find number of sides.

Method 1: Using formula
Each angle = [(n - 2) × 180°] / n 140 = [(n - 2) × 180] / n
140n = 180n - 360
40n = 360
n = 9

The polygon has 9 sides (nonagon)
Example 17: Using exterior angle Each interior angle = 140° Each exterior angle = 180° - 140° = 40°
n = 360° / 40° = 9 sides

J. PROBLEM SOLVING

Example 18: A polygon has 12 sides. Find: a) Sum of interior angles b) Each interior angle if regular c) Each exterior angle if regular
Solution:
a) Sum = (12 - 2) × 180°
= 10 × 180°
= 1,800°

Each interior angle = 1,800° / 12
= 150°

Each exterior angle = 360° / 12
= 30°
OR: 180° - 150° = 30°
Example 19: Four angles of a pentagon are 100°, 110°, 105°, and 95°. Find the fifth angle.

Solution:
Sum of interior angles = (5 - 2) × 180° = 540°

Sum of 4 angles = 100° + 110° + 105° + 95° = 410°

Fifth angle = 540° - 410° = 130°

Example 20: In a hexagon, three angles are each 130° and two are each 110°. Find the sixth angle.
Solution:
Sum = (6 - 2) × 180° = 720°

Known angles = 3(130°) + 2(110°)
= 390° + 220°
= 610°

Sixth angle = 720° - 610° = 110°
Example 21: Each exterior angle of a regular polygon is 24°. How many sides does it have? Solution:
n = 360° / exterior angle n = 360° / 24°
n = 15 sides
Example 22: Can a regular polygon have each interior angle of 140°? Check:
Exterior angle = 180° - 140° = 40°
n = 360° / 40° = 9 ✓

Yes, it's a regular nonagon (9 sides)
Example 23: Can a regular polygon have each interior angle of 170°?

Check:
Exterior angle = 180° - 170° = 10°
n = 360° / 10° = 36 ✓

Yes, it's a 36-sided polygon

WEEK 3: ANGLES OF ELEVATION AND DEPRESSION

TOPIC: Definition and Applications; Solving Problems Using Scale Drawings

CONTENT:
ANGLE OF ELEVATION

Definition: The angle of elevation is the angle between the horizontal line and the line of sight when looking UP at an object.
Key Components:

Object (top of building, plane, etc.)
/|
/ |
/ | Height
/θ |
/————|
Observer Horizontal line

θ = Angle of elevation

Important Points:

Measured from horizontal upward
Always between 0° and 90°
Observer looks UP
Forms right-angled triangle

Example 1: Standing 50 m from a building, you look up at 30° to see the top.

Top

Height (h)
30°

50 m

Angle of elevation = 30° Distance = 50 m
Real-Life Examples:

Looking up at a tall building
Watching an airplane in the sky
Observing a bird on a tree
Viewing the top of a mountain
Looking at a flag on a pole

ANGLE OF DEPRESSION

Definition: The angle of depression is the angle between the horizontal line and the line of sight when looking DOWN at an object.
Key Components:

Observer • ————— Horizontal line (on top) \θ
\
\
\
\
Object (on ground)

θ = Angle of depression

Important Points:
Measured from horizontal downward
Always between 0° and 90°
Observer looks DOWN
Equal to angle of elevation from object to observer

Example 2: From a 40 m high tower, you look down at 25° to see a car.

Tower top • ————— Horizontal
\25°
h=\40m
\
\
Car

Angle of depression = 25° Height = 40 m
Real-Life Examples:

Looking down from a building
Viewing ground from an airplane
Watching from a lighthouse
Observing from a bridge
Looking down from a hill

RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN ANGLES
Key Principle: Angle of elevation = Angle of depression (alternate angles) Observer B (high) • ————— Horizontal
\θ₂
\
\
\θ₁
Observer A (low) ————— Horizontal

θ₁ = Angle of elevation (from A to B) θ₂ = Angle of depression (from B to A)
θ₁ = θ₂ (alternate angles on parallel lines)

Example 3: A person on ground looks up at plane at 40°. What's the angle of depression from plane to person?
Answer: 40° (they are equal)

COMPONENTS OF RIGHT-ANGLED TRIANGLE

Height
Hypotenuse
θ

Adjacent

Opposite = Height (opposite to angle) Adjacent = Horizontal distance Hypotenuse = Line of sight
θ = Angle of elevation or depression

Trigonometric Ratios (Introduction):

tan θ = Opposite / Adjacent = Height / Distance
sin θ = Opposite / Hypotenuse
cos θ = Adjacent / Hypotenuse

SOLVING PROBLEMS USING SCALE DRAWING

Method:

Understand the problem
Draw a diagram (not to scale)
Choose appropriate scale
Draw accurate scale diagram
Measure required angle/distance
Calculate actual value

Example 4: A person stands 30 m from a building. The angle of elevation to the top is 35°. Find the height using scale drawing.
Solution:

Step 1: Draw rough diagram

Top
/ |
/	| h
/	|
/35° |
/———| 30 m
Step 2: Choose scale Scale: 1 cm = 5 m
Step 3: Calculate drawing dimensions Horizontal distance = 30 m
Drawing distance = 30 ÷ 5 = 6 cm
Step 4: Construct scale drawing

Draw horizontal line 6 cm
Use protractor to mark 35° angle
Draw line from angle
Draw vertical from end of horizontal to meet angled line
Measure vertical height in drawing

Step 5: Measure and calculate

Suppose vertical measures 4.2 cm Actual height = 4.2 × 5 = 21 m

Example 5: From top of 50 m building, angle of depression to a car is 28°. Find distance of car from building using scale drawing.
Solution: Diagram:
Building • —————
\28°
50m\
\
Car
d = ?

Scale: 1 cm = 10 m

Steps:

Vertical line 5 cm (represents 50 m)
Draw horizontal from top
Mark 28° below horizontal
Draw line to ground level
Measure horizontal distance
Calculate actual distance If measured distance = 9.4 cm Actual distance = 9.4 × 10 = 94 m

Example 6: A ladder 10 m long leans against a wall. The angle it makes with ground is 65°. Find height reached on wall.
Solution:

Diagram:

Wall |\
| \ 10 m (ladder) h |	\
|	\
|65° \
|_	\ Scale: 1 cm = 2 m Steps:
Draw line 5 cm (represents 10 m ladder)
Make 65° angle with horizontal
Draw vertical wall
Measure height reached
Calculate actual height If measured = 4.5 cm
Actual height = 4.5 × 2 = 9 m

PRACTICAL APPLICATIONS

Application 1: Measuring Tree Height

Stand at known distance from tree, measure angle of elevation to top, calculate height.

Example 7: Standing 20 m from tree, angle of elevation is 42°. Estimate height.

Application 2: Navigation

Ships and planes use angles of depression to find distances to landmarks.

Example 8: From lighthouse 60 m high, ship is spotted at angle of depression 15°. How far is ship?
Application 3: Construction

Architects use angles to design ramps, roofs, staircases.

Example 9: A ramp must rise 2 m over 8 m horizontal distance. Find angle.

Application 4: Surveying

Land surveyors measure distances and heights using angles.

STEP-BY-STEP PROBLEM SOLVING

Problem Type 1: Finding Height
Example 10: Distance from building = 40 m, angle of elevation = 38°. Find height. Given:
Adjacent (distance) = 40 m
Angle = 38°
Find: Opposite (height)

Scale Drawing:
Choose scale: 1 cm = 5 m
Draw horizontal: 40 ÷ 5 = 8 cm
Construct 38° angle
Complete triangle
Measure height: suppose 6.2 cm
Calculate: 6.2 × 5 = 31 m Height = 31 m (approximately)

Problem Type 2: Finding Distance
Example 11: Building height = 60 m, angle of depression = 32°. Find distance from building. Given:
Opposite (height) = 60 m
Angle of depression = 32° (= angle of elevation from ground)
Find: Adjacent (distance)

Scale Drawing:
Scale: 1 cm = 10 m
Draw vertical: 60 ÷ 10 = 6 cm
From top, draw 32° below horizontal
Complete triangle
Measure horizontal: suppose 9.5 cm
Calculate: 9.5 × 10 = 95 m Distance = 95 m (approximately)

Problem Type 3: Finding Angle

Example 12: Person 50 m from building sees top at height 30 m. Find angle of elevation.
Given:
Adjacent = 50 m
Opposite = 30 m
Find: Angle

Scale Drawing:
Scale: 1 cm = 10 m
Horizontal: 5 cm
Vertical: 3 cm
Complete triangle
Measure angle with protractor: suppose 31° Angle of elevation = 31° (approximately)

COMBINED PROBLEMS

Example 13: Two buildings are 80 m apart. From top of first building (40 m high), angle of depression to top of second building is 15°. Find height of second building.
Diagram:
Building 1 (40m) • —————
\15°
\
Top of Building 2
|
| h₂
|
Ground ————————————————
80 m

Solution using scale drawing:
Scale: 1 cm = 10 m
Draw horizontal 8 cm (80 m)
Vertical line 4 cm at left (40 m)
From top, draw 15° depression angle
Mark where it intersects above second building base
Measure this height from ground
Calculate actual height

Suppose measured 3.5 cm from ground Height of second building = 3.5 × 10 = 35 m

Example 14: A plane is flying at 5,000 m altitude. Ground observer sees it at angle of elevation 62°. How far horizontally is plane from observer?
Given:
Height = 5,000 m
Angle of elevation = 62°
Find: Horizontal distance
Scale: 1 cm = 1,000 m Steps:
Vertical: 5 cm (5,000 m)
Construct 62° at base
Complete triangle
Measure horizontal: suppose 2.7 cm 5. Calculate: 2.7 × 1,000 = 2,700 m

Horizontal distance = 2,700 m or 2.7 km

QUANTITATIVE APTITUDE PROBLEMS

Example 15: A man walks 100 m away from base of tower. His angle of elevation to top decreases from 45° to 30°. Find height of tower.
Solution Strategy:

Draw both positions
Use same height for both triangles
Find relationship
Calculate height

Example 16: From ship, lighthouse top is seen at 35° elevation. After sailing 500 m directly toward it, angle is 55°. Find lighthouse height.
Solution: Two positions create two triangles sharing same height. Use scale drawing for each position.

J. ACCURACY AND ESTIMATION

Tips for Accurate Scale Drawings:

Use sharp pencil
Measure carefully
Use ruler and protractor properly
Choose convenient scale
Draw large enough for accuracy
Check measurements
Label clearly Common Errors to Avoid:
Wrong angle (elevation vs depression)
Wrong side as opposite/adjacent
Scale conversion mistakes
Inaccurate protractor reading
Forgetting to convert back to actual units

WEEK 4: BEARINGS AND DISTANCES I

TOPIC: Cardinal Points; Locating Positions; Finding Distances and Bearings Using Scale Drawing
CONTENT:
CARDINAL POINTS

Definition: Cardinal points are the four main directions used for navigation.

The Four Cardinal Points:

N (North)
↑
|
|
W (West) ←——•——→ E (East)
|
|
↓
S (South)

Key Facts:

North (N): 0° or 360°
East (E): 90°
South (S): 180°
West (W): 270°
Measured clockwise from North

INTERCARDINAL (ORDINAL) POINTS

Secondary Directions:

NW	N	NE
\ | /
\ | /
W ———— • ———— E
/ | \
/ | \
SW	S	SE

The Eight Main Directions:

DirectionAbbreviationBearing from NDescriptionNorthN0° (360°)CardinalNortheastNE45°IntercardinalEastE90°CardinalSoutheastSE135°IntercardinalSouthS180°CardinalSouthwestSW225°IntercardinalWestW270°CardinalNorthwestNW315°Intercardinal

THREE-FIGURE BEARINGS

Definition: A bearing is a direction measured clockwise from North, expressed as a three-figure number (000° to 360°).
Rules:
Always measured from North
Always clockwise
Always three figures (use leading zeros)
In degrees

Examples:

Example 1: 045° (Northeast direction)
Example 2: 090° (Due East)
Example 3: 180° (Due South)
Example 4: 270° (Due West)
Example 5: 315° (Northwest)

Converting Descriptions to Bearings: Example 6:
North = 000° or 360°
East = 090°
South = 180°
West = 270°

Example 7: Northeast

Halfway between N (0°) and E (90°)
Bearing = 045°

Example 8: Southeast

Halfway between E (90°) and S (180°)
Bearing = 135°

Example 9: Southwest

Halfway between S (180°) and W (270°)
Bearing = 225°

Example 10: Northwest

Halfway between W (270°) and N (360°)
Bearing = 315°

MEASURING BEARINGS Method 1: Using Compass Directions Example 11: "30° East of North"
N
|	/
| /30°
|/
E

Bearing = 030°
Example 12: "40° South of East" N
|
|
W ———•——— E
\
\40°
\ S

From North clockwise:
90° + 40° = 130°
Bearing = 130°
Example 13: "25° West of South" From North clockwise:
180° + 25° = 205°
Bearing = 205°
Example 14: "50° North of West" From North clockwise:
270° + 50° = 320°
Wait - this goes past North! Actually: 270° - 50° = 220°
OR: From North: 360° - 50° = 310°

Check the direction description carefully!
"50° North of West" means starting from West, go 50° toward North 270° + 50° = 320°... but that's past North
Correct: 270° - 50° = 220°? No...

Actually, "North of West" means from West going toward North
West = 270°, going toward North (which would decrease the angle going clockwise, or we think counterclockwise from West)
Better: 360° - 50° = 310° ✓

Bearing = 310°

Method 2: Back Bearings

Definition: The back bearing (or return bearing) is the bearing from B to A when you know the bearing from A to B.
Rule:

If bearing from A to B < 180°: Back bearing = Bearing + 180°
If bearing from A to B > 180°: Back bearing = Bearing - 180°

Example 15: Bearing from A to B is 060°. Find bearing from B to A. N
|	/
A | /60°
| /
•————
|B
|

Bearing A to B = 060° Since 060° < 180°:
Bearing B to A = 060° + 180° = 240°
Example 16: Bearing from P to Q is 215°. Find bearing from Q to P. Since 215° > 180°:
Bearing Q to P = 215° - 180° = 035°
Example 17: Bearing from X to Y is 130°. Find bearing from Y to X. Since 130° < 180°:
Bearing Y to X = 130° + 180° = 310°

LOCATING POSITIONS

Using Bearings and Distances:

To locate a point, you need:

Starting point (reference)
Bearing (direction)
Distance

Example 18: From point A, point B is on bearing 070° and 50 km away. Locate B.

Steps:

Mark point A
Draw North line from A
Measure 70° clockwise from North
Mark scale (e.g., 1 cm = 10 km)
Measure 5 cm in direction of 070°
Mark point B

|	/
A | /70°
|/
•

/ B (50km away)
/5cm on scale 1cm=10km

Example 19: From town T, village V is on bearing 215° and distance 30 km. Locate V.
Choose scale: 1 cm = 5 km Drawing distance = 30 ÷ 5 = 6 cm

Steps:
Mark T
Draw North line
Measure 215° clockwise
Mark 6 cm along this direction
Mark point V

FINDING DISTANCES USING SCALE DRAWING

Example 20: Town A is 40 km North of town B. Town C is 30 km East of B. Find distance from A to C.
Solution:

Scale: 1 cm = 10 km

Steps:

Mark point B
Draw North line
Mark A 4 cm North of B (40 km)
Mark C 3 cm East of B (30 km)
Join A to C
Measure AC in drawing
Calculate actual distance A •
|
|4cm (40km)
|
B •———————• C
3cm (30km)

By measurement: AC ≈ 5 cm (on drawing) Actual distance = 5 × 10 = 50 km

OR by calculation (Pythagoras):
AC² = 40² + 30² = 1,600 + 900 = 2,500
AC = 50 km ✓

Example 21: Ship A is 60 km on bearing 030° from port P. Ship B is 80 km on bearing 120° from P. Find distance between ships using scale drawing.
Solution:

Scale: 1 cm = 20 km

Ship A: 60 ÷ 20 = 3 cm at 030°
Ship B: 80 ÷ 20 = 4 cm at 120°

Steps:
Mark port P
Draw North line
Draw line at 030°, mark A at 3 cm
Draw line at 120°, mark B at 4 cm
Join A to B
Measure AB
Calculate actual distance

Suppose AB measures 6.5 cm Actual distance = 6.5 × 20 = 130 km

FINDING BEARINGS USING SCALE DRAWING

Example 22: Town P is 50 km North of town Q. Town R is 40 km East of Q. Find bearing of R from P.
Solution:
Scale: 1 cm = 10 km P •
|
|5cm
|
Q •———————• R
4cm

Steps:
Draw the configuration
Draw North line from P
Join P to R
Measure angle from North (clockwise) to PR
Suppose angle measures 129° Bearing of R from P = 129°

Example 23: From lighthouse L, boat A is 25 km on bearing 040°. Boat B is 35 km on bearing 150°. Find bearing of B from A.
Solution:

Scale: 1 cm = 5 km
Steps:
Mark L, draw North
Mark A at 5 cm on 040°
Mark B at 7 cm on 150°
Draw North line from A
Join A to B
Measure angle from North at A to AB (clockwise)
Read bearing

Suppose angle = 168° Bearing of B from A = 168°

PRACTICAL NAVIGATION PROBLEMS

Problem Type 1: Three-Point Navigation

Example 24: A hiker walks 8 km on bearing 060° from camp C to point P, then 6 km on bearing 150° to point Q. Find: a) Distance from C to Q b) Bearing of Q from C
Solution:
Scale: 1 cm = 2 km Steps:
Mark C
Draw North, mark P at 4 cm on 060°
From P, draw North, mark Q at 3 cm on 150°
Join C to Q, measure distance and bearing

Results (by measurement):
Suppose CQ = 6 cm → Actual = 12 km
Suppose bearing = 094° → Bearing of Q from C = 094°

Problem Type 2: Return Journey

Example 25: Airplane flies 200 km on bearing 135° from airport A to city B. What bearing should it use to return to A?
Bearing A to B = 135° Since 135° < 180°:
Bearing B to A = 135° + 180° = 315°

Problem Type 3: Shortest Distance

Example 26: Town A is 50 km due North of town B. Town C is 40 km due East of A. A person at B wants to reach C. Find shortest distance.
Solution:

C •
|40km
|
B •———————• A
50km

By scale drawing or Pythagoras:
BC² = BA² + AC² = 50² + 40² = 2,500 + 1,600 = 4,100 BC = √4,100 ≈ 64 km

COMPASS BEARINGS VS THREE-FIGURE BEARINGS Compass Bearings (Old Method): Examples: N30°E, S40°W, N60°W Three-Figure Bearings (Modern Method): Examples: 030°, 220°, 300° Conversion:
Example 27: Convert N30°E to three-figure bearing

Start from North, go 30° toward East Three-figure bearing = 030°
Example 28: Convert S40°E to three-figure bearing

Start from South (180°), go 40° toward East Three-figure bearing = 180° - 40° = 140°
Example 29: Convert N50°W to three-figure bearing

Start from North, go 50° toward West Three-figure bearing = 360° - 50° = 310°
Example 30: Convert three-figure bearing 240° to compass bearing

240° is past South (180°) toward West (270°)
240° - 180° = 60°
Compass bearing = S60°W

TOPIC: Constructing Triangles; Bisecting Angles

CONTENT:
TRIANGLE CONSTRUCTIONS

Definition: Triangle construction means drawing an accurate triangle given certain measurements.
Three Standard Cases:

Three sides (SSS)
Two sides and included angle (SAS)
Two angles and included side (ASA)

CONSTRUCTION CASE 1: THREE SIDES (SSS)

When Given: All three side lengths

Example 1: Construct triangle ABC where AB = 7 cm, BC = 6 cm, AC = 5 cm

Steps:

Draw base AB = 7 cm

A •————————————• B
7 cm

With compass point at A, radius 5 cm, draw arc above AB A •~~~~arc (5cm radius)
With compass point at B, radius 6 cm, draw arc to intersect first arc
B •~~~~arc (6cm radius)

Mark intersection point as C
C

5cm	6cm

A	B
7 cm

Join A to C and B to C Complete triangle ABC

Example 2: Construct triangle PQR with sides 8 cm, 6 cm, 4 cm

Steps:

Draw longest side as base: PQ = 8 cm
From P: arc radius 6 cm
From Q: arc radius 4 cm
Mark intersection R
Join to complete triangle

Triangle Inequality Check: Before construction, verify:

Sum of any two sides > third side
8 + 6 > 4 ✓
8 + 4 > 6 ✓
6 + 4 > 8 ✓ Valid triangle

Example 3: Can you construct triangle with sides 3 cm, 4 cm, 8 cm?

Check: 3 + 4 = 7, which is NOT > 8 Invalid - cannot construct this triangle
The two shorter sides must add to more than the longest side

CONSTRUCTION CASE 2: TWO SIDES AND INCLUDED ANGLE (SAS)

Invalid - cannot construct this triangle Sum of angles must equal 180°

SPECIAL TRIANGLES

Equilateral Triangle: All sides equal, all angles 60°

Construction (given side 6 cm):

Draw base 6 cm
From each end, draw arcs radius 6 cm
Mark intersection
Complete triangle

Isosceles Triangle: Two sides equal, two angles equal

Right-Angled Triangle: One angle = 90°

Example 10: Construct right-angled triangle with legs 5 cm and 4 cm

Steps:

Draw one leg horizontal: 5 cm
At one end, construct 90° angle (perpendicular)
Mark 4 cm on perpendicular
Join to complete triangle

BISECTING ANGLES

Definition: Angle bisection means dividing an angle into two equal parts.

Method Using Compass: Example 11: Bisect angle of 80° Steps:
Draw angle ABC = 80°

A •
\
\80°
\

With B as center, any convenient radius, draw arc cutting both arms A •

B •————• C
arc

\ × (intersection)
\/
B •————• C
